========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, doctor. Thanks for seeing me. Um, I hope I'm doing this right, but... I guess I'm here because I've just been getting a little overwhelmed lately. It's, um... it's about my face. I know it probably sounds really vain or silly, so I hate to even bring it up, but I just can't stop thinking that the left side of my face looks completely crooked and... just messed up. 

I try not to make a big deal out of it, but it feels like everyone at the warehouse where I work, or even when I try to go out to salsa night, is noticing it and staring. They tell me they don't see anything when I ask, which is really nice of them to say, but... I don't know. It's just always on my mind and it's making things hard. Does that make sense? I hope that's an okay reason to be here.

I mean... I guess there's a possibility? I really want to agree with you, doctor, because I know you see things much more clearly than I do. It would honestly be a huge relief if it was just a thought that wasn't real.

I mean... I really want to say it's just a feeling. Because I know that's the logical answer, right? That's probably what I should be telling you. But... honestly, I just can't stop thinking that the left side of my face actually looks crooked and... well, messed up.

But even when they say it looks fine, I just can't get it out of my head that it's physically crooked and they're just being polite.

I mean... I want to say yes? Because when you put it like that, it sounds like the right way to look at it. And I really want to be on the same page as you, because I know you're the expert here and you're trying to help me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess... I mean, yes, I know people tell me it's fine, and I know I should probably count that as evidence. My mom, my coworkers... they all say they don't see it. And I really, really want to believe them. I don't want to seem like I'm just ignoring what everyone says, or like I'm calling them liars. I know they're trying to be supportive.

But... it's just so hard when I look in the mirror and I see it right there. It feels like the evidence is on my face, you know? But I know that's probably the disorder talking, like you were saying before. I am trying to listen to the evidence from other people, I really am. I hope that's okay? I don't want you to think I'm just completely unreasonable or totally ignoring reality. I'm trying to balance what people tell me with what I see... am I making any sense?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thanks for seeing me. Um, I hope I'm doing this right, but... I guess I'm here because I've just been getting a little overwhelmed lately. It's, um... it's about my face. I know it probably sounds really vain or silly, so I hate to even bring it up, but I just can't stop thinking that the left side of my face looks completely crooked and... just messed up. \n\nI try not to make a big deal out of it, but it feels like everyone at the warehouse where I work, or even when I try to go out to salsa night, is noticing it and staring. They tell me they don't see anything when I ask, which is really nice of them to say, but... I don't know. It's just always on my mind and it's making things hard. Does that make sense? I hope that's an okay reason to be here.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the left side of your face actually looks normal, and it's just your perception of it that's off?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess maybe? I really want to believe that, doctor, I do. It would be so much easier if it was just in my head. And I know you're the expert, so if you think that's a possibility, I'm definitely open to it. \n\nIt's just... when I look in the mirror, it looks so obviously wrong to me. But I know my mind can play tricks on me sometimes. Do you think that's what's happening? I don't want to seem stubborn or like I'm not listening to you. I really am trying to understand. Am I making sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any chance that others are staring at you because they're actually talking about or noticing something else, rather than your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I mean, you're probably exactly right. I try to tell myself that all the time. Like, maybe I just made a loud noise moving a pallet at the warehouse, or... or maybe they're just spacing out in my direction and I happen to be standing there. \n\nI really don't want to be one of those guys who thinks everyone is always focused on him, you know? That would be so self-centered, and I really try not to be like that. So I do try to tell myself they're looking at literally anything else. \n\nIt's just... in the moment, I get so anxious and I just immediately assume it's my face because it's so... well, because of how it looks to me. But I'm sure you make a really good point, doctor. I probably just need to try harder to remember that they have their own stuff going on. I hope I don't sound too crazy or paranoid saying all this? I really appreciate you talking it through with me.\n\nDoctor: Does your perception of your face being crooked change at all throughout the day, or does it always look the same way to you?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess it doesn't really change much? I mean, I try not to look in the mirror too often because I know I shouldn't be obsessing over it, but... whenever I do catch a glimpse, like in the bathroom at work or something, it always just looks the same to me. Always just... off. \n\nI really wish I could say it gets better on good days, because I know I should be trying to stay positive. And I do try, I promise! But it just sort of... stays looking crooked to me, morning or night. Is that... is that normal for someone feeling like this? I hope I'm answering your question right. I just want to give you the information you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face actually is perfectly normal and that the idea it's crooked is a thought that's not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess there's a possibility? I really want to agree with you, doctor, because I know you see things much more clearly than I do. It would honestly be a huge relief if it was just a thought that wasn't real. \n\nI try to tell myself that sometimes. Like, maybe I'm just being silly and making a big deal out of nothing. I really don't want to be someone who just refuses to listen to reason, you know? So I do try to entertain the idea that it's perfectly normal and I'm just... mistaken. \n\nIt's just hard to hold onto that thought when I actually see it, but I promise I'm trying to be open-minded about it. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not coming across as too difficult. I really do value your opinion on this.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually is crooked, or is it just that you feel like it is?\n\nPatient: I mean... I really want to say it's just a feeling. Because I know that's the logical answer, right? That's probably what I should be telling you. But... honestly, I just can't stop thinking that the left side of my face actually looks crooked and... well, messed up. \n\nIt's actually the whole reason I'm here. It's like, I go to work at the warehouse, or I go out to salsa night, and I just feel like everyone is noticing it. And I know people try to be nice—they tell me they don't see anything when I bring it up. I really try to believe them, I promise I do! I don't want to be calling them liars or anything. But even when they say it looks fine, I just can't get it out of my head that it's physically crooked and they're just being polite. \n\nI know that probably sounds so stubborn of me. I'm really trying to see it the way you and everyone else does. Am I making any sense at all? I hope I'm not frustrating you by saying I still think it's really there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to be mistaken about your face being crooked, or do you feel absolutely certain that it's true?\n\nPatient: I mean... I know I should say I could be mistaken. I really, really want to be mistaken. And I try to tell myself that I am, because I know that's what you're trying to help me see, and I really appreciate that. I don't want to be difficult or seem like I'm ignoring your expertise.\n\nBut... deep down? When I'm just alone with my thoughts? I feel pretty certain that it's crooked. I'm sorry, I know that's probably not what I'm supposed to say. It just feels so real to me, you know? Like, I can see it. But I am trying to work on it, I promise. Is it okay that I still feel this way? I don't... I don't really know. I want to say yes, absolutely, because I know that's the healthy way to think. I really want to be open to being convinced. If you or someone else could just show me proof, I'd love to just let it go and be normal.

That would be so self-centered, and I really try not to be like that. So I do try to tell myself they're looking at literally anything else. \n\nIt's just... in the moment, I get so anxious and I just immediately assume it's my face because it's so... well, because of how it looks to me. But I'm sure you make a really good point, doctor. I probably just need to try harder to remember that they have their own stuff going on. I hope I don't sound too crazy or paranoid saying all this? I really appreciate you talking it through with me.\n\nDoctor: Does your perception of your face being crooked change at all throughout the day, or does it always look the same way to you?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess it doesn't really change much? I mean, I try not to look in the mirror too often because I know I shouldn't be obsessing over it, but... whenever I do catch a glimpse, like in the bathroom at work or something, it always just looks the same to me. Always just... off. \n\nI really wish I could say it gets better on good days, because I know I should be trying to stay positive. And I do try, I promise! But it just sort of... stays looking crooked to me, morning or night. Is that... is that normal for someone feeling like this? I hope I'm answering your question right. I just want to give you the information you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face actually is perfectly normal and that the idea it's crooked is a thought that's not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess there's a possibility? I really want to agree with you, doctor, because I know you see things much more clearly than I do. It would honestly be a huge relief if it was just a thought that wasn't real. \n\nI try to tell myself that sometimes. Like, maybe I'm just being silly and making a big deal out of nothing. I really don't want to be someone who just refuses to listen to reason, you know? So I do try to entertain the idea that it's perfectly normal and I'm just... mistaken. \n\nIt's just hard to hold onto that thought when I actually see it, but I promise I'm trying to be open-minded about it. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not coming across as too difficult. I really do value your opinion on this.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually is crooked, or is it just that you feel like it is?\n\nPatient: I mean... I really want to say it's just a feeling. Because I know that's the logical answer, right? That's probably what I should be telling you. But... honestly, I just can't stop thinking that the left side of my face actually looks crooked and... well, messed up. \n\nIt's actually the whole reason I'm here. It's like, I go to work at the warehouse, or I go out to salsa night, and I just feel like everyone is noticing it. And I know people try to be nice—they tell me they don't see anything when I bring it up. I really try to believe them, I promise I do! I don't want to be calling them liars or anything. But even when they say it looks fine, I just can't get it out of my head that it's physically crooked and they're just being polite. \n\nI know that probably sounds so stubborn of me. I'm really trying to see it the way you and everyone else does. Am I making any sense at all? I hope I'm not frustrating you by saying I still think it's really there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to be mistaken about your face being crooked, or do you feel absolutely certain that it's true?\n\nPatient: I mean... I know I should say I could be mistaken. I really, really want to be mistaken. And I try to tell myself that I am, because I know that's what you're trying to help me see, and I really appreciate that. I don't want to be difficult or seem like I'm ignoring your expertise.\n\nBut... deep down? When I'm just alone with my thoughts? I feel pretty certain that it's crooked. I'm sorry, I know that's probably not what I'm supposed to say. It just feels so real to me, you know? Like, I can see it. But I am trying to work on it, I promise. Is it okay that I still feel this way? I hope I'm not being a bad patient by not just agreeing right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else would be able to see that your face is crooked if they looked closely?\n\nPatient: I mean... I think so? Or, well, I guess I *feel* like they can. I know I mentioned this earlier, but it's really the whole reason I'm here. I just... I can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. \n\nWhen I'm at work at the warehouse, or if I go to salsa night, I just feel like everyone is staring at it. And I know people say they don't see anything, and I really don't want to think they're lying to me. I try so hard to just take their word for it, because I want to be agreeable and not make a fuss. But I just keep thinking that if they really looked closely, they'd see it, and they're just being nice to me. \n\nI'm sorry, I know I'm probably repeating myself. I just... I want to give you the right answer, but that's honestly how it feels to me. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding too crazy when I say that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that might be a false idea that you've gotten stuck on?\n\nPatient: I mean... I want to say yes? Because when you put it like that, it sounds like the right way to look at it. It feels so real. \n\nI'm sorry, I know I should probably just agree with you that it's a false idea. I want to be a good patient and make progress. I really am trying to see it your way, I promise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked is something that you believe despite evidence to the contrary?\n\nPatient: I guess... I mean, yes, I know people tell me it's fine, and I know I should probably count that as evidence. My mom, my coworkers... they all say they don't see it. And I really, really want to believe them. I don't want to seem like I'm just ignoring what everyone says, or like I'm calling them liars. I know they're trying to be supportive.\n\nBut... it's just so hard when I look in the mirror and I see it right there. It feels like the evidence is on my face, you know? But I know that's probably the disorder talking, like you were saying before. I am trying to listen to the evidence from other people, I really am. I hope that's okay? I don't want you to think I'm just completely unreasonable or totally ignoring reality. I'm trying to balance what people tell me with what I see... am I making any sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could convince you that your face is not actually crooked?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't really know. I want to say yes, absolutely, because I know that's the healthy way to think. I really want to be open to being convinced. If you or someone else could just show me proof, I'd love to just let it go and be normal. \n\nBut I'm honestly not sure what that evidence would even look like. Like, if a plastic surgeon measured it and showed me the numbers? Maybe? But then I worry I'd just think they measured it wrong, or that they were just trying to make me feel better, like everyone else. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that sounds terrible. I know I should be more open-minded about this. I'm really trying not to be closed off to getting better. Is it bad that I don't know what would convince me? I hope I'm not being too difficult of a patient by saying that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I want to say it's just my imagination, I really do. I know that's probably what it is, right? Since you and everyone else are telling me it's not real. I don't want to sound crazy or like I'm completely disconnected from reality.

I know it probably sounds really vain or silly, so I hate to even bring it up, but I just can't stop thinking that the left side of my face looks completely crooked and... just messed up. \n\nI try not to make a big deal out of it, but it feels like everyone at the warehouse where I work, or even when I try to go out to salsa night, is noticing it and staring. I really don't want to be someone who just refuses to listen to reason, you know? So I do try to entertain the idea that it's perfectly normal and I'm just... mistaken. \n\nIt's just hard to hold onto that thought when I actually see it, but I promise I'm trying to be open-minded about it. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not coming across as too difficult. I really do value your opinion on this.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually is crooked, or is it just that you feel like it is?\n\nPatient: I mean... I really want to say it's just a feeling. But... honestly, I just can't stop thinking that the left side of my face actually looks crooked and... well, messed up. \n\nIt's actually the whole reason I'm here. It's like, I go to work at the warehouse, or I go out to salsa night, and I just feel like everyone is noticing it. But even when they say it looks fine, I just can't get it out of my head that it's physically crooked and they're just being polite. \n\nI know that probably sounds so stubborn of me. I'm really trying to see it the way you and everyone else does. Am I making any sense at all? I hope I'm not frustrating you by saying I still think it's really there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to be mistaken about your face being crooked, or do you feel absolutely certain that it's true?\n\nPatient: I mean... I know I should say I could be mistaken. I really, really want to be mistaken. I don't want to be difficult or seem like I'm ignoring your expertise.\n\nBut... deep down? When I'm just alone with my thoughts? I feel pretty certain that it's crooked. I'm sorry, I know that's probably not what I'm supposed to say. It just feels so real to me, you know? Like, I can see it. But I am trying to work on it, I promise. Is it okay that I still feel this way? I hope I'm not being a bad patient by not just agreeing right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else would be able to see that your face is crooked if they looked closely?\n\nPatient: I mean... I think so? Or, well, I guess I *feel* like they can. I know I mentioned this earlier, but it's really the whole reason I'm here. I just... I can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. \n\nWhen I'm at work at the warehouse, or if I go to salsa night, I just feel like everyone is staring at it. And I know people say they don't see anything, and I really don't want to think they're lying to me. I try so hard to just take their word for it, because I want to be agreeable and not make a fuss. But I just keep thinking that if they really looked closely, they'd see it, and they're just being nice to me. \n\nI'm sorry, I know I'm probably repeating myself. I just... I want to give you the right answer, but that's honestly how it feels to me. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding too crazy when I say that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that might be a false idea that you've gotten stuck on?\n\nPatient: I mean... I want to say yes? Because when you put it like that, it sounds like the right way to look at it. And I really want to be on the same page as you, because I know you're the expert here and you're trying to help me. \n\nBut... I don't know if I can honestly say it's just a false idea. I'll stand in front of the mirror and say, \"It's just in your head, it's just a false idea you're stuck on.\" But then I look at my face and... it just looks so crooked to me. I don't want to seem like I'm just ignoring what everyone says, or like I'm calling them liars. I know they're trying to be supportive.\n\nBut... it's just so hard when I look in the mirror and I see it right there. It feels like the evidence is on my face, you know? I don't want you to think I'm just completely unreasonable or totally ignoring reality. I'm trying to balance what people tell me with what I see... am I making any sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could convince you that your face is not actually crooked?\n\nPatient: I don't... I know I should say I could be mistaken. I really, really want to be mistaken. And I try to tell myself that I am, because I know that's what you're trying to help me see, and I really appreciate that. I don't want to be difficult or seem like I'm ignoring your expertise.\n\nBut... deep down? When I'm just alone with my thoughts? I feel pretty certain that it's crooked. I'm sorry, I know that's probably not what I'm supposed to say. It just feels so real to me, you know? Like, I can see it. But I am trying to work on it, I promise. Is it okay that I still feel this way? I hope I'm not being a bad patient by not just agreeing right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else would be able to see that your face is crooked if they looked closely?\n\nPatient: I mean... I think so? Or, well, I guess I *feel* like they can. I know I mentioned this earlier, but it's really the whole reason I'm here. I just... I can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. \n\nWhen I'm at work at the warehouse, or if I go to salsa night, I just feel like everyone is staring at it. And I know people say they don't see anything, and I really don't want to think they're lying to me. I try so hard to just take their word for it, because I want to be agreeable and not make a fuss. I want to give you the right answer, but that's honestly how it feels to me. Does that make sense? I want to say yes? Because when you put it like that, it sounds like the right way to look at it. And I really want to be on the same page as you, because I know you're the expert here and you're trying to help me. \n\nBut... I don't know if I can honestly say it's just a false idea. I try to tell myself it is, I really do! I'll stand in front of the mirror and say, \"It's just in your head, it's just a false idea you're stuck on.\" But then I look at my face and... it just looks so crooked to me. It feels so real. \n\nI'm sorry, I know I should probably just agree with you that it's a false idea. I want to be a good patient and make progress. Is it okay that I'm still struggling to see it that way? I hope I'm not disappointing you by being so stubborn about this. I really am trying to see it your way, I promise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked is something that you believe despite evidence to the contrary?\n\nPatient: I guess... I mean, yes, I know people tell me it's fine, and I know I should probably count that as evidence. My mom, my coworkers... they all say they don't see it. And I really, really want to believe them. I don't want to seem like I'm just ignoring what everyone says, or like I'm calling them liars. I know they're trying to be supportive.\n\nBut... it's just so hard when I look in the mirror and I see it right there. It feels like the evidence is on my face, you know? I don't want you to think I'm just completely unreasonable or totally ignoring reality. I want to say yes? Because I know that's the healthy way to think, right? Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... gosh, I'd really like to think so. I mean, if a whole group of doctors looked at me and told me I was fine, I should definitely believe them, right? Because you guys went to school for this and you know exactly what you're talking about. I really want to be the kind of person who just listens to the experts and, you know, moves on. 

But... I honestly just don't know. I feel terrible even admitting that to you. It's just... since I'm already having such a hard time shaking this, even with you sitting right here helping me, I'm not sure if having more doctors say it would magically fix it in my head. 

I'm so sorry, I know that probably sounds incredibly disrespectful to your profession, and I promise I don't mean it that way at all. I have so much respect for doctors. I just... I really don't know for sure if it would change how I see myself in the mirror. Is it okay that I don't know the answer to that? I'm really trying to be a good patient for you, I promise.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thanks for seeing me. Um, I hope I'm doing this right, but... I guess I'm here because I've just been getting a little overwhelmed lately. It's, um... it's about my face. I know it probably sounds really vain or silly, so I hate to even bring it up, but I just can't stop thinking that the left side of my face looks completely crooked and... just messed up. \n\nI try not to make a big deal out of it, but it feels like everyone at the warehouse where I work, or even when I try to go out to salsa night, is noticing it and staring. They tell me they don't see anything when I ask, which is really nice of them to say, but... I don't know. It's just always on my mind and it's making things hard. Does that make sense? I hope that's an okay reason to be here.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the left side of your face actually looks normal, and it's just your perception of it that's off?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess maybe? I really want to believe that, doctor, I do. It would be so much easier if it was just in my head. And I know you're the expert, so if you think that's a possibility, I'm definitely open to it. \n\nIt's just... when I look in the mirror, it looks so obviously wrong to me. But I know my mind can play tricks on me sometimes. Do you think that's what's happening? I don't want to seem stubborn or like I'm not listening to you. I really am trying to understand. Am I making sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any chance that others are staring at you because they're actually talking about or noticing something else, rather than your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I mean, you're probably exactly right. I try to tell myself that all the time. Like, maybe I just made a loud noise moving a pallet at the warehouse, or... or maybe they're just spacing out in my direction and I happen to be standing there. \n\nI really don't want to be one of those guys who thinks everyone is always focused on him, you know? That would be so self-centered, and I really try not to be like that. So I do try to tell myself they're looking at literally anything else. \n\nIt's just... in the moment, I get so anxious and I just immediately assume it's my face because it's so... well, because of how it looks to me. But I'm sure you make a really good point, doctor. I probably just need to try harder to remember that they have their own stuff going on. I hope I don't sound too crazy or paranoid saying all this? I really appreciate you talking it through with me.\n\nDoctor: Does your perception of your face being crooked change at all throughout the day, or does it always look the same way to you?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess it doesn't really change much? I mean, I try not to look in the mirror too often because I know I shouldn't be obsessing over it, but... whenever I do catch a glimpse, like in the bathroom at work or something, it always just looks the same to me. Always just... off. \n\nI really wish I could say it gets better on good days, because I know I should be trying to stay positive. And I do try, I promise! But it just sort of... stays looking crooked to me, morning or night. Is that... is that normal for someone feeling like this? I hope I'm answering your question right. I just want to give you the information you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face actually is perfectly normal and that the idea it's crooked is a thought that's not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess there's a possibility? I really want to agree with you, doctor, because I know you see things much more clearly than I do. It would honestly be a huge relief if it was just a thought that wasn't real. \n\nI try to tell myself that sometimes. Like, maybe I'm just being silly and making a big deal out of nothing. I really don't want to be someone who just refuses to listen to reason, you know? So I do try to entertain the idea that it's perfectly normal and I'm just... mistaken. \n\nIt's just hard to hold onto that thought when I actually see it, but I promise I'm trying to be open-minded about it. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not coming across as too difficult. I really do value your opinion on this.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually is crooked, or is it just that you feel like it is?\n\nPatient: I mean... I really want to say it's just a feeling. Because I know that's the logical answer, right? That's probably what I should be telling you. But... honestly, I just can't stop thinking that the left side of my face actually looks crooked and... well, messed up. \n\nIt's actually the whole reason I'm here. It's like, I go to work at the warehouse, or I go out to salsa night, and I just feel like everyone is noticing it. And I know people try to be nice—they tell me they don't see anything when I bring it up. I really try to believe them, I promise I do! I don't want to be calling them liars or anything. But even when they say it looks fine, I just can't get it out of my head that it's physically crooked and they're just being polite. \n\nI know that probably sounds so stubborn of me. I'm really trying to see it the way you and everyone else does. Am I making any sense at all? I hope I'm not frustrating you by saying I still think it's really there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to be mistaken about your face being crooked, or do you feel absolutely certain that it's true?\n\nPatient: I mean... I know I should say I could be mistaken. I really, really want to be mistaken. And I try to tell myself that I am, because I know that's what you're trying to help me see, and I really appreciate that. I don't want to be difficult or seem like I'm ignoring your expertise.\n\nBut... deep down? When I'm just alone with my thoughts? I feel pretty certain that it's crooked. I'm sorry, I know that's probably not what I'm supposed to say. It just feels so real to me, you know? Like, I can see it. But I am trying to work on it, I promise. Is it okay that I still feel this way? I hope I'm not being a bad patient by not just agreeing right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else would be able to see that your face is crooked if they looked closely?\n\nPatient: I mean... I think so? Or, well, I guess I *feel* like they can. I know I mentioned this earlier, but it's really the whole reason I'm here. I just... I can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. \n\nWhen I'm at work at the warehouse, or if I go to salsa night, I just feel like everyone is staring at it. And I know people say they don't see anything, and I really don't want to think they're lying to me. I try so hard to just take their word for it, because I want to be agreeable and not make a fuss. But I just keep thinking that if they really looked closely, they'd see it, and they're just being nice to me. \n\nI'm sorry, I know I'm probably repeating myself. I just... I want to give you the right answer, but that's honestly how it feels to me. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding too crazy when I say that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that might be a false idea that you've gotten stuck on?\n\nPatient: I mean... I want to say yes? Because when you put it like that, it sounds like the right way to look at it. And I really want to be on the same page as you, because I know you're the expert here and you're trying to help me. \n\nBut... I don't know if I can honestly say it's just a false idea. I try to tell myself it is, I really do! I'll stand in front of the mirror and say, \"It's just in your head, it's just a false idea you're stuck on.\" But then I look at my face and... it just looks so crooked to me. It feels so real. \n\nI'm sorry, I know I should probably just agree with you that it's a false idea. I want to be a good patient and make progress. Is it okay that I'm still struggling to see it that way? I hope I'm not disappointing you by being so stubborn about this. I really am trying to see it your way, I promise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked is something that you believe despite evidence to the contrary?\n\nPatient: I guess... I mean, yes, I know people tell me it's fine, and I know I should probably count that as evidence. My mom, my coworkers... they all say they don't see it. And I really, really want to believe them. I don't want to seem like I'm just ignoring what everyone says, or like I'm calling them liars. I know they're trying to be supportive.\n\nBut... it's just so hard when I look in the mirror and I see it right there. It feels like the evidence is on my face, you know? But I know that's probably the disorder talking, like you were saying before. I am trying to listen to the evidence from other people, I really am. I hope that's okay? I don't want you to think I'm just completely unreasonable or totally ignoring reality. I'm trying to balance what people tell me with what I see... am I making any sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could convince you that your face is not actually crooked?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't really know. I want to say yes, absolutely, because I know that's the healthy way to think. I really want to be open to being convinced. If you or someone else could just show me proof, I'd love to just let it go and be normal. \n\nBut I'm honestly not sure what that evidence would even look like. Like, if a plastic surgeon measured it and showed me the numbers? Maybe? But then I worry I'd just think they measured it wrong, or that they were just trying to make me feel better, like everyone else. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that sounds terrible. I know I should be more open-minded about this. I'm really trying not to be closed off to getting better. Is it bad that I don't know what would convince me? I hope I'm not being too difficult of a patient by saying that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that your face is crooked is something that you might be completely wrong about, even if it feels true to you?\n\nPatient: I mean... I want to say yes? Because I know that's what I'm supposed to say, right? Like, I know you're trying to help me see that my brain might be playing tricks on me. And I really, really want to agree with you. I want to be the kind of patient who can just step back and say, \"Okay, I'm completely wrong about this.\" \n\nBut... it's just so hard. Even when I try to force myself to think, \"You're wrong, it's fine,\" it still feels so incredibly real when I look in the mirror. I don't want to sound like I'm arguing with you, I promise I'm not. I know you're the doctor. I just... I struggle to fully believe I could be *completely* wrong when I see it every single day. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that's probably not the answer you're looking for. I am trying to keep an open mind, I really am. Am I doing this wrong? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information so you can help me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that your face is crooked is based on reality or is it a product of your imagination?\n\nPatient: I want to say it's just my imagination, I really do. I know that's probably what it is, right? Since you and everyone else are telling me it's not real. I don't want to sound crazy or like I'm completely disconnected from reality. \n\nBut... the whole reason I'm here is because I just can't stop thinking about it. I look in the mirror and I see the left side of my face looking all crooked and messed up. And it's on my mind all the time. When I'm at work at the warehouse, or if I go to salsa night, I just feel like everyone is staring at it and noticing how uneven it is. Even when they tell me they don't see anything, my brain just won't let it go. \n\nI know I shouldn't be obsessing over it like this, and I'm really trying not to. I hope it doesn't make me seem totally irrational? I just want to be honest with you about how much it's bothering me, even if it is just my imagination playing tricks on me. Is that helpful? I just want to make sure I'm explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face is normal and the idea that it's crooked is a false belief that you can't change, no matter what anyone else says?\n\nPatient: I... gosh, I really want to just say yes. I know it would be so much better if I could just agree and say it's a completely false belief. I want to change how I see it, I really don't want to be this way, but when people tell me my face is normal... it just doesn't click in my head. I still see the crookedness no matter how hard I try to look past it. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound too stubborn, or like I'm just refusing to listen to you. I promise I'm not trying to be closed-minded. I'm really trying to see it your way. Am I making any sense at all? I just want to make sure I'm not being too difficult of a patient by not having a better answer for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen or anyone who could tell you something that would make you completely change your mind about your face being crooked?\n\nPatient: I mean... I want to say yes? Because I know that's the healthy way to think, right? Like, if someone showed me some kind of scientific proof, or if a doctor like you told me enough times, I *should* be able to just snap out of it and say, \"Oh, okay, I was wrong.\" \n\nBut... honestly? I just don't know if anything would actually work. I've had people at work, and friends at salsa night, tell me they don't see it. I think so? Or, well, I guess I *feel* like they can. I know I mentioned this earlier, but it's really the whole reason I'm here. I just... I want to give you the right answer, but that's honestly how it feels to me. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding too crazy when I say that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that might be a false idea that you've gotten stuck on?\n\nPatient: I mean... I want to say yes? Because when you put it like that, it sounds like the right way to look at it. And I really want to be on the same page as you, because I know you're the expert here and you're trying to help me. \n\nBut... I don't know if I can honestly say it's just a false idea. It feels so real. \n\nI'm sorry, I know I should probably just agree with you that it's a false idea. I want to be a good patient and make progress. Is it okay that I'm still struggling to see it that way? I hope I'm not disappointing you by being so stubborn about this. I hope I'm not being too difficult of a patient by saying that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that your face is crooked is something that you might be completely wrong about, even if it feels true to you?\n\nPatient: I mean... I know I should say I could be mistaken. I really, really want to be mistaken. And I try to tell myself that I am, because I know that's what you're trying to help me see, and I really appreciate that. I don't want to be difficult or seem like I'm ignoring your expertise.\n\nBut... deep down? When I'm just alone with my thoughts? I feel pretty certain that it's crooked. I'm sorry, I know that's probably not what I'm supposed to say. It just feels so real to me, you know? Like, I can see it. But I am trying to work on it, I promise. Is it okay that I still feel this way? I hope I'm not being a bad patient by not just agreeing right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else would be able to see that your face is crooked if they looked closely?\n\nPatient: I mean... I think so? Or, well, I guess I *feel* like they can. I know I mentioned this earlier, but it's really the whole reason I'm here. I just... I can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. \n\nWhen I'm at work at the warehouse, or if I go to salsa night, I just feel like everyone is staring at it. And I know people say they don't see anything, and I really don't want to think they're lying to me. I try so hard to just take their word for it, because I want to be agreeable and not make a fuss. But I just keep thinking that if they really looked closely, they'd see it, and they're just being nice to me. \n\nI'm sorry, I know I'm probably repeating myself. I just... I want to give you the right answer, but that's honestly how it feels to me. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding too crazy when I say that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that might be a false idea that you've gotten stuck on?\n\nPatient: I mean... I want to say yes? Because when you put it like that, it sounds like the right way to look at it. And I really want to be on the same page as you, because I know you're the expert here and you're trying to help me. \n\nBut... I don't know if I can honestly say it's just a false idea. I try to tell myself it is, I really do! I'll stand in front of the mirror and say, \"It's just in your head, it's just a false idea you're stuck on.\" But then I look at my face and... it just looks so crooked to me. It feels so real. \n\nI'm sorry, I know I should probably just agree with you that it's a false idea. I want to be a good patient and make progress. Is it okay that I'm still struggling to see it that way? I hope I'm not disappointing you by being so stubborn about this. I really am trying to see it your way, I promise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked is something that you believe despite evidence to the contrary?\n\nPatient: I guess... I mean, yes, I know people tell me it's fine, and I know I should probably count that as evidence. My mom, my coworkers... they all say they don't see it. And I really, really want to believe them. I don't want to seem like I'm just ignoring what everyone says, or like I'm calling them liars. I know they're trying to be supportive.\n\nBut... it's just so hard when I look in the mirror and I see it right there. It feels like the evidence is on my face, you know? But I know that's probably the disorder talking, like you were saying before. I am trying to listen to the evidence from other people, I really am. I hope that's okay? I don't want you to think I'm just completely unreasonable or totally ignoring reality. I'm trying to balance what people tell me with what I see... am I making any sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could convince you that your face is not actually crooked?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't really know. I want to say yes, absolutely, because I know that's the healthy way to think. I really want to be open to being convinced. If you or someone else could just show me proof, I'd love to just let it go and be normal. \n\nBut I'm honestly not sure what that evidence would even look like. Like, if a plastic surgeon measured it and showed me the numbers? Maybe? But then I worry I'd just think they measured it wrong, or that they were just trying to make me feel better, like everyone else. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that sounds terrible. I know I should be more open-minded about this. I'm really trying not to be closed off to getting better. Is it bad that I don't know what would convince me? I hope I'm not being too difficult of a patient by saying that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that your face is crooked is something that you might be completely wrong about, even if it feels true to you?\n\nPatient: I mean... I want to say yes? Because I know that's what I'm supposed to say, right? Like, I know you're trying to help me see that my brain might be playing tricks on me. And I really, really want to agree with you. I want to be the kind of patient who can just step back and say, \"Okay, I'm completely wrong about this.\" \n\nBut... it's just so hard. Even when I try to force myself to think, \"You're wrong, it's fine,\" it still feels so incredibly real when I look in the mirror. I don't want to sound like I'm arguing with you, I promise I'm not. I know you're the doctor. I just... I struggle to fully believe I could be *completely* wrong when I see it every single day. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that's probably not the answer you're looking for. I am trying to keep an open mind, I really am. Am I doing this wrong? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information so you can help me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that your face is crooked is based on reality or is it a product of your imagination?\n\nPatient: I want to say it's just my imagination, I really do. I know that's probably what it is, right? Since you and everyone else are telling me it's not real. I don't want to sound crazy or like I'm completely disconnected from reality. \n\nBut... the whole reason I'm here is because I just can't stop thinking about it. I look in the mirror and I see the left side of my face looking all crooked and messed up. And it's on my mind all the time. When I'm at work at the warehouse, or if I go to salsa night, I just feel like everyone is staring at it and noticing how uneven it is. Even when they tell me they don't see anything, my brain just won't let it go. \n\nI know I shouldn't be obsessing over it like this, and I'm really trying not to. I hope it doesn't make me seem totally irrational? I just want to be honest with you about how much it's bothering me, even if it is just my imagination playing tricks on me. Is that helpful? I just want to make sure I'm explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face is normal and the idea that it's crooked is a false belief that you can't change, no matter what anyone else says?\n\nPatient: I... gosh, I really want to just say yes. I know it would be so much better if I could just agree and say it's a completely false belief. I want to be able to do that for you, because I know you're trying to help me see reason. \n\nBut I honestly just don't know. It's like my brain is stuck. I want to change how I see it, I really don't want to be this way, but when people tell me my face is normal... it just doesn't click in my head. I still see the crookedness no matter how hard I try to look past it. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound too stubborn, or like I'm just refusing to listen to you. I promise I'm not trying to be closed-minded. I'm really trying to see it your way. Am I making any sense at all? I just want to make sure I'm not being too difficult of a patient by not having a better answer for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen or anyone who could tell you something that would make you completely change your mind about your face being crooked?\n\nPatient: I mean... I want to say yes? Because I know that's the healthy way to think, right? Like, if someone showed me some kind of scientific proof, or if a doctor like you told me enough times, I *should* be able to just snap out of it and say, \"Oh, okay, I was wrong.\" \n\nBut... honestly? I just don't know if anything would actually work. I've had people at work, and friends at salsa night, tell me they don't see it. I honestly just don't know. I feel terrible even admitting that to you. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... a delusion? Wow, that's a really strong word. I mean, when I think of a delusion, I think of someone who's completely lost touch with reality, you know? Like seeing things that aren't there at all, or hearing voices. 

I really don't want you to think I'm crazy or anything. I know I'm struggling with this, and I know it takes up way too much of my time, but I promise I'm still grounded in reality. I still go to work at the warehouse every day, I do my job... I'm a normal guy, I swear. 

I guess... I mean, I know you're the doctor, and if you think it's a delusion, I'm sure you have a really good reason for saying that. I don't want to argue with you at all. It's just... it's hard for me to call it that because I feel like I'm just seeing a physical flaw that's actually there on my face, even if I am obsessing over it too much. 

Does that make sense? I'm so sorry if I'm being defensive, I really don't mean to be. I just... I want to make sure you don't think I'm completely out of my mind. Is it okay that I don't really see it as a delusion? I'll use whatever word you think is best, I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of what's going on with me.

But even when they say it looks fine, I just can't get it out of my head that it's physically crooked and they're just being polite. \n\nI know that probably sounds so stubborn of me. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thanks for seeing me. Um, I hope I'm doing this right, but... I guess I'm here because I've just been getting a little overwhelmed lately. It's, um... it's about my face. I know it probably sounds really vain or silly, so I hate to even bring it up, but I just can't stop thinking that the left side of my face looks completely crooked and... just messed up. \n\nI try not to make a big deal out of it, but it feels like everyone at the warehouse where I work, or even when I try to go out to salsa night, is noticing it and staring. That would be so self-centered, and I really try not to be like that. But I'm sure you make a really good point, doctor. I probably just need to try harder to remember that they have their own stuff going on. I hope I don't sound too crazy or paranoid saying all this? I really appreciate you talking it through with me.\n\nDoctor: Does your perception of your face being crooked change at all throughout the day, or does it always look the same way to you?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess it doesn't really change much? I mean, I try not to look in the mirror too often because I know I shouldn't be obsessing over it, but... whenever I do catch a glimpse, like in the bathroom at work or something, it always just looks the same to me. Always just... off. \n\nI really wish I could say it gets better on good days, because I know I should be trying to stay positive. And I do try, I promise! But it just sort of... stays looking crooked to me, morning or night. Is that... is that normal for someone feeling like this? I hope I'm answering your question right. I just want to give you the information you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face actually is perfectly normal and that the idea it's crooked is a thought that's not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess there's a possibility? I really want to agree with you, doctor, because I know you see things much more clearly than I do. It would honestly be a huge relief if it was just a thought that wasn't real. \n\nI try to tell myself that sometimes. Like, maybe I'm just being silly and making a big deal out of nothing. I really don't want to be someone who just refuses to listen to reason, you know? So I do try to entertain the idea that it's perfectly normal and I'm just... mistaken. \n\nIt's just hard to hold onto that thought when I actually see it, but I promise I'm trying to be open-minded about it. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not coming across as too difficult. I really do value your opinion on this.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually is crooked, or is it just that you feel like it is?\n\nPatient: I mean... I really want to say it's just a feeling. Because I know that's the logical answer, right? That's probably what I should be telling you. But... honestly, I just can't stop thinking that the left side of my face actually looks crooked and... well, messed up. \n\nIt's actually the whole reason I'm here. It's like, I go to work at the warehouse, or I go out to salsa night, and I just feel like everyone is noticing it. And I know people try to be nice—they tell me they don't see anything when I bring it up. I really try to believe them, I promise I do! I don't want to be calling them liars or anything. But even when they say it looks fine, I just can't get it out of my head that it's physically crooked and they're just being polite. \n\nI know that probably sounds so stubborn of me. I'm really trying to see it the way you and everyone else does. Am I making any sense at all? I hope I'm not frustrating you by saying I still think it's really there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to be mistaken about your face being crooked, or do you feel absolutely certain that it's true?\n\nPatient: I mean... I know I should say I could be mistaken. I really, really want to be mistaken. And I try to tell myself that I am, because I know that's what you're trying to help me see, and I really appreciate that. I don't want to be difficult or seem like I'm ignoring your expertise.\n\nBut... deep down? When I'm just alone with my thoughts? I feel pretty certain that it's crooked. I'm sorry, I know that's probably not what I'm supposed to say. It just feels so real to me, you know? Like, I can see it. But I am trying to work on it, I promise. Is it okay that I still feel this way? I hope I'm not being a bad patient by not just agreeing right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else would be able to see that your face is crooked if they looked closely?\n\nPatient: I mean... I can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. \n\nWhen I'm at work at the warehouse, or if I go to salsa night, I just feel like everyone is staring at it. I want to give you the right answer, but that's honestly how it feels to me. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding too crazy when I say that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that might be a false idea that you've gotten stuck on?\n\nPatient: I mean... I want to say yes? Because when you put it like that, it sounds like the right way to look at it. It feels so real. \n\nI'm sorry, I know I should probably just agree with you that it's a false idea. I want to be a good patient and make progress. Is it okay that I'm still struggling to see it that way? I hope I'm not disappointing you by being so stubborn about this. I really am trying to see it your way, I promise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked is something that you believe despite evidence to the contrary?\n\nPatient: I guess... I mean, yes, I know people tell me it's fine, and I know I should probably count that as evidence. My mom, my coworkers... they all say they don't see it. And I really, really want to believe them. I don't want to seem like I'm just ignoring what everyone says, or like I'm calling them liars. I know they're trying to be supportive.\n\nBut... it's just so hard when I look in the mirror and I see it right there. It feels like the evidence is on my face, you know? I don't want you to think I'm just completely unreasonable or totally ignoring reality. I'm trying to balance what people tell me with what I see... am I making any sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could convince you that your face is not actually crooked?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't really know. I want to say yes, absolutely, because I know that's the healthy way to think. I really want to be open to being convinced. If you or someone else could just show me proof, I'd love to just let it go and be normal. \n\nBut I'm honestly not sure what that evidence would even look like. Like, if a plastic surgeon measured it and showed me the numbers? Maybe? I know I should be more open-minded about this. I'm really trying not to be closed off to getting better. Is it bad that I don't know what would convince me? I hope I'm not being too difficult of a patient by saying that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that your face is crooked is something that you might be completely wrong about, even if it feels true to you?\n\nPatient: I mean... And I really, really want to agree with you. I want to be the kind of patient who can just step back and say, \"Okay, I'm completely wrong about this.\" \n\nBut... it's just so hard. Even when I try to force myself to think, \"You're wrong, it's fine,\" it still feels so incredibly real when I look in the mirror. I don't want to sound like I'm arguing with you, I promise I'm not. I know you're the doctor. I just... I struggle to fully believe I could be *completely* wrong when I see it every single day. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that's probably not the answer you're looking for. I am trying to keep an open mind, I really am. Am I doing this wrong? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information so you can help me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that your face is crooked is based on reality or is it a product of your imagination?\n\nPatient: I want to say it's just my imagination, I really do. Even when they tell me they don't see anything, my brain just won't let it go. \n\nI know I shouldn't be obsessing over it like this, and I'm really trying not to. I hope it doesn't make me seem totally irrational? I just want to be honest with you about how much it's bothering me, even if it is just my imagination playing tricks on me. Is that helpful? I just want to make sure I'm explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face is normal and the idea that it's crooked is a false belief that you can't change, no matter what anyone else says?\n\nPatient: I... gosh, I really want to just say yes. I know it would be so much better if I could just agree and say it's a completely false belief. I want to be able to do that for you, because I know you're trying to help me see reason. \n\nBut I honestly just don't know. It's like my brain is stuck. I want to change how I see it, I really don't want to be this way, but when people tell me my face is normal... it just doesn't click in my head. I still see the crookedness no matter how hard I try to look past it. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound too stubborn, or like I'm just refusing to listen to you. I promise I'm not trying to be closed-minded. I'm really trying to see it your way. Am I making any sense at all? I just want to make sure I'm not being too difficult of a patient by not having a better answer for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen or anyone who could tell you something that would make you completely change your mind about your face being crooked?\n\nPatient: I mean... I want to say yes? Because I know that's the healthy way to think, right? Like, if someone showed me some kind of scientific proof, or if a doctor like you told me enough times, I *should* be able to just snap out of it and say, \"Oh, okay, I was wrong.\" \n\nBut... honestly? I just don't know if anything would actually work. I've had people at work, and friends at salsa night, tell me they don't see it. And I nod and say thank you, because I don't want to make them uncomfortable or seem like I'm calling them liars. But deep down, the second I look in a mirror again, it's just... right there. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that sounds awful. I really don't want to be one of those people who just refuses to listen to reason or won't let anyone help them. I promise I would *want* to change my mind if someone could prove it to me. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not coming across as impossible to treat... I really am trying my best to work with you on this.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked would change even if you received confirmation from multiple medical professionals that it's perfectly normal?\n\nPatient: I... gosh, I'd really like to think so. I mean, if a whole group of doctors looked at me and told me I was fine, I should definitely believe them, right? Because you guys went to school for this and you know exactly what you're talking about. I really want to be the kind of person who just listens to the experts and, you know, moves on. \n\nBut... I honestly just don't know. I feel terrible even admitting that to you. It's just... since I'm already having such a hard time shaking this, even with you sitting right here helping me, I'm not sure if having more doctors say it would magically fix it in my head. \n\nI'm so sorry, I know that probably sounds incredibly disrespectful to your profession, and I promise I don't mean it that way at all. I have so much respect for doctors. I just... I really don't know for sure if it would change how I see myself in the mirror. Is it okay that I don't know the answer to that? I'm really trying to be a good patient for you, I promise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could be entirely mistaken about your face being crooked, with no basis in reality?\n\nPatient: I... I really want to say yes to that. I mean, I *want* to be entirely mistaken, you know? It would be such a relief to just find out that my brain is completely making this up and that everyone else is right. I know that's what you're hoping I can see, and I really, really want to get to that point for you. \n\nBut... gosh, when I look in the mirror, it just feels so real. I see the crookedness so clearly. So... I guess I just don't know if I could be *entirely* mistaken with absolutely zero basis in reality. I mean, maybe it's not as bad as I think it is? Maybe I'm blowing it out of proportion? I'd be more than happy to admit that. \n\nI'm sorry, I feel like I'm giving you the wrong answer again. I know you're trying to help me realize that it's all in my head, and I feel awful that I can't just agree with you completely. I hope I'm not being too frustrating... I really am trying to be open to what you're saying. Am I doing okay? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the answers you need to help me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that your face is crooked is something that you hold onto despite any evidence or reassurance to the contrary?\n\nPatient: I... I really hope it doesn't seem like I'm doing it on purpose. Like I'm just being stubborn and actively ignoring the evidence, you know? I promise I'm not trying to be difficult or dismissive of what people say. \n\nI don't really know if I'd say I \"hold onto it,\" because honestly, I would let it go in a heartbeat if I could. I really do try to take the reassurance to heart, especially when it comes from you. It's just... I guess my eyes just still see what they see when I'm by myself in the bathroom mirror, even after someone has just told me it's fine. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that probably means I'm not making the progress you want me to make. I really want to just be able to say, \"no, I don't hold onto it at all,\" but I want to be completely honest with you too so you can actually help me. I hope that's okay? I just really don't want you to think I'm ignoring your help or choosing to be stuck like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that your face is crooked is a delusion?\n\nPatient: I... a delusion? Wow, that's a really strong word. I mean, when I think of a delusion, I think of someone who's completely lost touch with reality, you know? Like seeing things that aren't there at all, or hearing voices. \n\nI really don't want you to think I'm crazy or anything. I know I'm struggling with this, and I know it takes up way too much of my time, but I promise I'm still grounded in reality. I still go to work at the warehouse every day, I do my job... I'm a normal guy, I swear. \n\nI guess... I mean, I know you're the doctor, and if you think it's a delusion, I'm sure you have a really good reason for saying that. I don't want to argue with you at all. It's just... it's hard for me to call it that because I feel like I'm just seeing a physical flaw that's actually there on my face, even if I am obsessing over it too much. \n\nDoes that make sense? I'm so sorry if I'm being defensive, I really don't mean to be. I just... I want to make sure you don't think I'm completely out of my mind. Is it okay that I don't really see it as a delusion? I'll use whatever word you think is best, I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of what's going on with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that you could ever change your mind about your face being crooked, no matter what evidence or reassurance you receive?\n\nPatient: I... I really, really hope so. I mean, I want to change my mind. I'd give anything to just wake up one day, look in the mirror, and see what everyone else says they see. I don't want to be stuck like this forever, worrying about my face and taking up your time.\n\nI guess... I mean, I know I've been really stubborn about it so far, and I'm so sorry if it seems like I'm ignoring the reassurance. But I want to believe there's a possibility. Maybe with your help, and if I keep working at it... maybe eventually something will just click? \n\nI really don't want to be a hopeless case, you know? I want to get better and be the kind of patient who actually improves. So, yes, I think there has to be a possibility. I'm willing to try whatever you suggest to get there. Is that... is that the right way to look at it? I hope I'm not sounding too crazy when I say that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that might be a false idea that you've gotten stuck on?\n\nPatient: I mean... I want to say yes? Because when you put it like that, it sounds like the right way to look at it. And I really want to be on the same page as you, because I know you're the expert here and you're trying to help me. \n\nBut... I don't know if I can honestly say it's just a false idea. I'll stand in front of the mirror and say, \"It's just in your head, it's just a false idea you're stuck on.\" But then I look at my face and... it just looks so crooked to me. It feels so real. \n\nI'm sorry, I know I should probably just agree with you that it's a false idea. I want to be a good patient and make progress. Is it okay that I'm still struggling to see it that way? I hope I'm not disappointing you by being so stubborn about this. I really am trying to see it your way, I promise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked is something that you believe despite evidence to the contrary?\n\nPatient: I guess... I don't want to seem like I'm just ignoring what everyone says, or like I'm calling them liars. I know they're trying to be supportive.\n\nBut... it's just so hard when I look in the mirror and I see it right there. It feels like the evidence is on my face, you know?
